EUROPEAN ORGANISATION FOR NUCLEAR RESEARCH (CERN)
Submitted to: Phys. Rev. C.
CERN-EP-2026-154
June 19, 2026
Measurements of charged-particle pseudorapidity
and transverse momentum distributions in O+O and
Ne+Ne collisions at √𝒔NN = 5.36 TeV with the ATLAS
detector
The ATLAS Collaboration
Measurements of charged-particle transverse momentum spectra, multiplicity, and mean
transverse momentum are presented as a function of pseudorapidity and collision centrality
in O+O and Ne+Ne collisions at √𝑠NN = 5.36 TeV using 27.7 𝜇b−1 and 53.1 𝜇b−1 data sets
recorded by the ATLAS experiment at the LHC. The collision centrality is characterized by
the total transverse energy measured in the ATLAS forward calorimeters. The kinematics of
charged particles are reconstructed with the inner detector over the fiducial pseudorapidity
and transverse momentum ranges |𝜂| < 2.5 and 0.27 < 𝑝T < 5 GeV using data from the
ATLAS inner detector. The per-event charged-particle pseudorapidity density 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and
mean transverse momentum ⟨𝑝T⟩are measured over this fiducial range as a function of 𝜂. The
results are reported in 5%-wide centrality intervals covering the 5–80% centrality range, and in
1%-wide intervals covering the 0–5% centrality range. Invariant per-event yields are evaluated
as a function of 𝜂and 𝑝T. Their 𝑝T dependence is fitted to estimate extrapolated 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and
⟨𝑝T⟩values over 0 < 𝑝T < 5 GeV. To evaluate the impact of using pseudorapidity instead of
rapidity, measurements are also performed as a function of rapidity computed using a pion
mass hypothesis. The fiducial and extrapolated results are compared with hydrodynamic
calculations. Ratios of the 𝑝T-differential yields between centrality intervals, and between
O+O and Ne+Ne collisions, are used to assess the centrality- and system-dependence of radial
flow developed in the collisions.

## Page 2


1 Introduction
Heavy-ion collisions at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) are understood to create highly out-of-equilibrium
strongly interacting matter at energy densities well in excess of 10 GeV/fm3 (see Ref. [1]). This matter
subsequently evolves within ≲1 fm/𝑐[2] into a “hydrodynamized”, although not yet completely thermalized,
quark–gluon plasma (QGP) [3, 4]. Viscous hydrodynamic calculations [5, 6] have been very successful in
describing a variety of soft single- and multi-hadron measurements at the Relativistic Heavy Ion Collider
(RHIC) and the LHC, but only if the ratio of shear viscosity to entropy density (𝜂/𝑠) in units of ℏis
∼0.1–0.2, close to the conjectured lower bound on 𝜂/𝑠[7–9]. Such small values require the mean free path
of the microscopic constituents of the plasma to be much smaller than the transverse size of the plasma
which is determined by the size of the colliding nuclei.
Extensive theoretical advances in the last two decades (see e.g. Refs. [2, 10]) have provided a sound footing
for understanding the rapid transition from the out-of-equilibrium initial state to the hydrodynamically
evolving plasma. The results of these advances also provide a rigorous connection between certain
final-state observables and the initial state. In particular, the density per unit rapidity and per unit transverse
area of charged particles produced in a heavy-ion collision is found to be closely related to the initial energy
density and to the entropy density in the plasma at the hydrodynamization time [11]. Such a connection
was originally proposed by Bjorken in the context of a specific hydrodynamic model [12], but it is found to
apply even for the strongly out-of-equilibrium initial conditions of ultrarelativistic heavy-ion collisions [11,
13, 14]. In addition, the mean transverse momentum of the produced particles is sensitive to the collective
transverse velocity or “radial flow” developed during the hydrodynamic expansion [15, 16].
A topic of great interest in the study of the QGP is whether there exists some transverse size scale below
which the above-described paradigm for the creation, hydrodynamization, and subsequent evolution of the
QGP becomes invalid. This question is made particularly urgent by the appearance of trends in single- and
multi-hadron data obtained from proton–proton [17–19] and proton–nucleus collisions [20–23] that are
nearly identical to those observed in heavy-ion collisions [24, 25], suggesting that QGP can be created with
a transverse size comparable to the size of a proton, i.e. comparable to the QCD confinement distance scale.
Systematic studies of multiple observables in collisions of nuclei of different size – since the nuclear size
also determines the maximum transverse size of the plasma – may provide important insight into whether
the paradigm described above for the creation and evolution of the QGP evolves smoothly with transverse
size down to the size of a proton. Considerations such as this have motivated interest in studying collisions
of nuclei much smaller than lead nuclei at the LHC [26].
In July of 2025, the LHC carried out a long-anticipated set of special runs that included O+O and Ne+Ne
collisions at a center-of-mass energy of 5.36 TeV per nucleon pair. The inclusion of Ne+Ne data-taking in
the special runs arose from recent strong interest in the heavy-ion community in studying the impact of
the shape of the colliding nuclei on the initial state, the subsequent hydrodynamic evolution of the QGP,
and on observables sensitive to that evolution [27–29]. The 16O and 20Ne nuclei have similar sizes, but
are expected to have different shapes, due to the role of alpha clustering [30] in the ground-state wave
functions. In particular, the 20Ne nucleus is thought to acquire a “bowling pin” shape due to the presence of
an additional alpha cluster compared to the 16O nucleus. Hydrodynamic calculations [31] that start from ab
initio calculations of the nuclear geometry [30, 32–34] and use the Trento model [35] to describe the initial
particle production and the energy-density distribution at the onset of hydrodynamic evolution, predict
measurable effects from nuclear-shape differences. Observables such as the mean transverse momentum
(⟨𝑝T⟩) and the Fourier coefficients of the azimuthal harmonics [36] (𝑣𝑛), especially 𝑣2, show sensitivity to
the different geometries of the 16O and 20Ne nuclei. Measurements of the azimuthal Fourier coefficients
2


## Page 3


of charged particles produced in O+O and Ne+Ne collisions [37–39] have yielded results consistent with
predictions arising from the hydrodynamic calculations. However, tests of other predictions from the
model, including the charged-particle pseudorapidity density and ⟨𝑝T⟩, are needed to verify both the initial
conditions of the calculations and their description of the radial flow developed during the evolution of the
plasma.
This paper presents measurements of charged-particle pseudorapidity density, transverse momentum, and
mean transverse momentum in O+O and Ne+Ne collisions, using data collected by ATLAS during the
aforementioned special runs of the LHC. The centrality of the O+O and Ne+Ne collisions is characterized
by the total transverse energy measured in the ATLAS forward calorimeters, and results are presented over
the centrality range 0–80% in both collision systems. Charged particles are reconstructed from hits in the
ATLAS inner detector over the “fiducial” pseudorapidity and transverse momentum intervals, |𝜂| < 2.5 and
0.27 < 𝑝T < 5 GeV, respectively. The measurements are performed as a function of 𝜂and rapidity, where
the latter is computed assuming the charged-pion mass hypothesis and denoted by 𝑦𝜋. The use of pion
rapidity provides results that are closer to those obtained using true rapidity, which is the natural variable
for describing particle production in hadronic interactions but is not accessible to ATLAS due to its limited
ability to identify hadrons. Results are presented for 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂,1 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦𝜋, and ⟨𝑝T⟩evaluated as a function
of 𝜂and 𝑦𝜋. Results are also presented for the double-differential distributions, (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂and
(1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝑦𝜋in different 𝜂or 𝑦𝜋intervals. Results for 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩are obtained both in
the fiducial kinematic range and with extrapolation to 𝑝T > 0 MeV using fits to the 𝑝T dependence of
(1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂and (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝑦𝜋. The measured 𝑝T spectra are compared between different
centrality intervals within the same collision system and between O+O and Ne+Ne collisions. The results
of the presented measurements are compared with predictions from hydrodynamic calculations.
The remainder of this paper is structured as follows: Section 2 describes the ATLAS experiment; Section 3
describes the data sets used in the analysis and the object reconstruction; Section 4 presents the analysis
methods; Section 5 discusses systematic uncertainties; Section 6 presents a subset of the results; and
Section 7 summarizes the paper and discusses conclusions drawn from the measurements. The appendix
presents more results that could not be included in Section 6 without detracting from its readability.
2 ATLAS detector
The ATLAS experiment [40, 41] at the LHC comprises a nearly hermetic central detector surrounding
the collision point2 and special-purpose forward detectors including the zero-degree calorimeters (ZDCs)
that are described below. The central detector consists of an inner tracking detector surrounded by a thin
superconducting solenoid, electromagnetic and hadronic calorimeters covering the region |𝜂| < 3.2 and
forward calorimeters (FCals) covering 3.2 < |𝜂| < 4.9, and a muon spectrometer incorporating three large
superconducting air-core toroidal magnets.
The inner-detector system (ID) is immersed in a 2 T axial magnetic field and provides charged-particle
tracking in the range |𝜂| < 2.5. A high-granularity silicon pixel detector covers the vertex region and
typically provides four measurements per track, the first hit generally being in the insertable B-layer (IBL)
1 Throughout this paper, when discussing differential distributions of particle yields (versus 𝑝T or 𝜂) the lower case “n” denotes
yield per event. Thus, e.g. 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂≡(1/𝑁evt)𝑑𝑁/𝑑𝜂.
2 ATLAS uses a right-handed coordinate system with its origin at the nominal interaction point (IP) in the center of the detector
and the 𝑧-axis along the beam pipe. The 𝑥-axis points from the IP to the center of the LHC ring, and the 𝑦-axis points upwards.
Polar coordinates (𝑟, 𝜙) are used in the transverse plane, 𝜙being the azimuthal angle around the 𝑧-axis. The pseudorapidity is
defined in terms of the polar angle 𝜃as 𝜂= −ln tan(𝜃/2) and is equal to the rapidity 𝑦= (1/2) ln[(𝐸+ 𝑝𝑧)/(𝐸−𝑝𝑧)] in the
relativistic limit. Angular distance is measured in units of Δ𝑅≡
√︁
(Δ𝑦)2 + (Δ𝜙)2.
3


## Page 4


installed before Run 2 [42, 43]. It is surrounded by a silicon microstrip tracker (the SCT), which usually
provides eight measurements per track. These silicon detectors are complemented by a transition radiation
tracker (TRT), which enables radially extended track reconstruction up to |𝜂| = 2.0.
The FCals [44] measure transverse energy in the forward region and are used to characterize the centrality of
heavy-ion collisions. Each FCal consists of three modules longitudinal in shower depth, one electromagnetic
and two hadronic, with dense absorber matrices and narrow liquid-argon gaps, optimized to measure the
large particle flux in the forward region.
Two ZDCs [45] are located at Δ𝑧= ±140 m from the center of ATLAS, within the LHC “Target Absorbers
for Neutrals” (TANs) shielding components. The ZDCs measure neutral particles produced with |𝜂| ≳8.4.
In heavy-ion collisions, these are primarily spectator neutrons that did not participate in the collision and
were emitted at very forward rapidities.
The luminosity is measured mainly by the LUCID-2 [46] detector, which records Cherenkov light produced
in the quartz windows of photomultipliers located close to the beam pipe.
ATLAS uses a two-level trigger system [47] consisting of a Level-1 (L1) trigger that is implemented using
dedicated electronics and a software-based “high-level trigger” (HLT) running algorithms similar to those
used in offline analysis. The Athena software suite [48] is used in data simulation, in the reconstruction and
analysis of real and simulated data, in detector operations, and in the trigger and data acquisition systems
of the experiment.
3 Data sets and object reconstruction
The O+O data used in this analysis were collected during a dedicated period of low-pileup operation, where
the mean number of interactions per bunch crossing was ⟨𝜇⟩∼0.01. The Ne+Ne data were collected in a
single LHC fill with ⟨𝜇⟩varying between 0.04 and 0.16. Minimum-bias events were selected using an
L1 trigger based on the TRT “FastOR” algorithm [49], together with an HLT requirement of at least one
reconstructed charged-particle track with 𝑝T > 200 MeV. The events used in the analysis were recorded
during stable LHC running conditions and have no detector hardware or readout errors [50]. The integrated
luminosities of O+O and Ne+Ne collisions sampled by the TRT trigger and passing event-cleaning
requirements (described below) are 27.7 𝜇b−1 and 53.1 𝜇b−1, respectively.
Charged-particle tracks and collision vertices are reconstructed from hits in the ID using standard
methods [51–54]. For the nominal analysis, the reconstructed tracks are required to have |𝜂| < 2.5 and
at least one pixel hit, with an additional requirement of a hit in the IBL or a hit in the next-to-innermost
pixel layer when one is expected.3 The tracks are required to have at least six SCT hits. To suppress
secondary-track contributions, the transverse impact parameter of the track with respect to the beamline,
𝑑0, and the longitudinal impact parameter of the track relative to the primary vertex [52, 53], 𝑧0 sin(𝜃), are
required to satisfy |𝑑0| < 1.5 mm and |𝑧0 sin(𝜃)| < 1.5 mm. An alternative set of track selections is used to
evaluate systematic uncertainties in the measurement. Like the nominal selection, the alternative selection
requires |𝜂| < 2.5, |𝑑0| < 1.5 mm, and |𝑧0 sin(𝜃)| < 1.5 mm, but its hit requirements are markedly different.
The track is required to have at least eight silicon-detector hits and at most two silicon-detector holes, with
3 A hit is expected if the extrapolated track crosses an active region of a silicon-sensor module (pixel or SCT) that has not been
disabled. A hit is said to be “missing” when it is expected but not found. A missing hit is called a hole. The hole counting does
not consider layers before the first hit or after the last hit. If a track crosses a disabled module, then for the purposes of hit
counting, the disabled module is counted as a hit. If a hit is used in reconstructing more than one track trajectory, it is called a
“shared hit”.
4


## Page 5


at most one hole in the pixel detector, at most one shared pixel hit or at most two shared SCT hits. If a track
has shared hits, then the number of required silicon-detector hits is increased to ten.
The 𝑧-position of the primary vertex [52, 53] is required to lie within 10 cm of the center of ATLAS. In
O+O and Ne+Ne collisions, backgrounds from ultraperipheral collisions (UPCs) are suppressed relative to
Pb+Pb collisions, but they still occur at an observable rate. The dominant UPC background in O+O and
Ne+Ne collisions is inclusive photonuclear (𝛾+ 𝐴) scattering, which produces asymmetric events with
little energy emitted in the hemisphere of the photon-emitting nucleus. A selection that requires at least
one topocluster [55] having 𝑝T > 400 MeV within the acceptance of each FCal is used to reject these
events. An additional requirement rejects events in which the fraction of all reconstructed tracks that satisfy
the aforementioned nominal selections is less than 50%. Such events are mostly associated with upstream
interactions and collisions involving “satellite” ions. In the recorded O+O and Ne+Ne collision data, there
are contributions from pileup events, in which two or more inelastic collisions occur in the same bunch
crossing. Most of the pileup events are removed by requiring exactly one high-quality reconstructed vertex
per event. A high-quality vertex is defined as one that is well-constrained in position, having a 𝑧-position
variance of less than 0.02 mm2. Pileup events are further suppressed by exploiting the correlation between
energy recorded in the FCal (Σ𝐸FCAL
T
) and the energy deposited in the ZDCs. The residual pileup after
these selections is estimated to be negligible.
As in earlier ATLAS studies of heavy-ion collisions, events are categorized into centrality percentiles
based on the Σ𝐸FCAL
T
[37, 56–58]. To relate the Σ𝐸FCAL
T
distribution to the sampled fraction of the
total inelastic O+O and Ne+Ne cross-sections, a Glauber-model-based calculation [59, 60] is used to fit
the data, to extract the fraction of events selected above a minimum Σ𝐸FCAL
T
threshold, and to estimate
the systematic uncertainty of that fraction [56–58]. Additionally, the Glauber model is used to extract
primary collision characteristics, such as the average number of participating nucleons, ⟨𝑁part⟩, for each
centrality interval. The ⟨𝑁part⟩values and their uncertainties corresponding to the centrality intervals
used here are shown in Table 1 in the Appendix. The Glauber analysis used in this paper represents an
evolution of that used in the ATLAS measurement of collective flow in O+O and Ne+Ne collisions [37].
It explicitly accounts for potential 𝛼-particle clustering in 16O and 20Ne nuclei by using nucleon density
distributions obtained from the PGCM [30, 33] and NLEFT [61] initial-state models as implemented in
the Trajectum hydrodynamic simulation code [62] (TR_OXYGEN_V15 and TR_NEON_V14 with 𝜔= 0.3).
The Σ𝐸FCAL
T
values corresponding to boundaries of O+O and Ne+Ne centrality intervals have changed
relative to Ref. [37] by less than 1% in the most central collisions and as much as 5% in the most peripheral
collisions.
The detector performance is studied using a sample of 50 million minimum-bias O+O Monte Carlo (MC)
events, corresponding to inclusive inelastic collisions without a bias toward a specific hard process, modeled
with the HIJING v1.3b event generator [63]. The generated events were passed through a full simulation
of the ATLAS detector [64] using Geant4 [65], and reconstructed using the same algorithms which are
applied to the data. Three alternative MC samples were produced using geometry variations that contain
additional material in either the IBL, the remainder of the ID, or the pixel-detector services in the forward
region. These are used to assess systematic uncertainties in the measurement that result from the limited
accuracy of the detector-material model used in the simulation.

4.1 Fiducial measurements
In this analysis, primary charged particles are those with a mean lifetime greater than 0.3×10−10 s, produced
either directly in the nucleus–nucleus interaction or in decays of particles with shorter lifetimes [66].
For primary charged particles, the efficiency (𝜀) of the track reconstruction procedure and subsequent
selections is determined using the HIJING MC sample. The same sample is used to determine the
misreconstructed-track5 (“fake-track”) fraction ( 𝑓fake) and the secondary-track fraction ( 𝑓sec) among the
selected tracks. The latter arise mainly from weak decays of hadrons or interactions with detector material.
The 𝜀, 𝑓fake, and 𝑓sec values are obtained after using standard ATLAS procedures [51, 54] to match
reconstructed tracks to primary charged particles generated by HIJING. Because of potential sensitivity of
the reconstruction to detector occupancy, the 𝜀, 𝑓fake, and 𝑓fake values are determined separately in intervals
of reconstructed charged-particle multiplicity 𝑁rec
chg computed over |𝜂| < 2.5 and 0.4 < 𝑝T < 5 GeV.6 The
use of multiplicity instead of centrality allows the same corrections to be applied to O+O and Ne+Ne data
and avoids potential complications in attempting to match HIJING and O+O or Ne+Ne centralities.
When evaluating 𝜀, 𝑓fake, and 𝑓sec, per-particle weights are applied to MC events to correct for 𝑝T-spectrum
differences between data and simulation and to adjust the kaon and proton fractions of the total particle yield
as a function of 𝑝T to match measurements by ALICE in Xe+Xe collisions [67]. These “flavor weights” are
applied because the tracking efficiency depends on the particle-species composition. The flavor reweighting
is performed in a centrality-independent manner, using the ALICE measurements of the kaon-to-pion
and proton-to-pion ratios in the 50–60% Xe+Xe centrality interval to correct the corresponding HIJING
fractions. The 50–60% centrality Xe+Xe collisions are chosen because the multiplicity in this interval
roughly corresponds to that in 0–10% centrality O+O collisions. To evaluate the systematic uncertainties
associated with the flavor reweighting, alternative weights derived from the 70–90% and 40–50% Xe+Xe
centrality intervals measured by ALICE are also applied. The reweighting factor depends on 𝑝T and is
∼1–1.6 for kaons and ∼0.5–1.4 for protons. To account for higher secondary-track rates due to enhanced
4 Here “invariant” refers to invariance under longitudinal boosts. Strictly speaking, only the (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝑦distribution is
invariant, whereas (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂and (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝑦𝜋are not.
5 These are tracks that do not correspond to true charged particles but arise from combinatorial hits in the detector.
6 A lower bound is placed on 𝑝T in the multiplicity calculation to match the definition in previous ATLAS measurements [37].

strange hadron production [67], a 𝑝T-independent weight of 1.5, motivated by the maximum reweighting
factor for kaons, is applied to secondary tracks from 𝐾s, Λ, and Σ decays.
A data-driven procedure is used to verify the fake-track fraction and improve the secondary-track fraction
estimates obtained from the MC simulation. This procedure takes advantage of the fact that the distributions
of distance of closest approach of a track to the beamline, 𝑑0, are observed in MC events to have significantly
different shapes for reconstructed primary particles, fake tracks, and secondary tracks, especially at low 𝑝T
where the fake-track fractions are largest. Template fits to measured 𝑑0 distributions are performed in three
𝜂intervals and in the 𝑝T bins used in the analysis. These yield 𝑓fake values within a few tenths of a percent
of those obtained from simulation, but 𝑓sec values larger than those obtained from simulation. The ratios of
the template-fit and MC-evaluated secondary-track rates obtained in the three 𝜂intervals are then applied
as per-secondary-particle weights in the simulation. These weights vary from near unity at low 𝑝T to a
maximum value between 2 and 2.5 near 𝑝T = 2 GeV, and then decrease with increasing 𝑝T. No significant
centrality dependence is observed in the template-fit results, so the applied secondary-track reweighting
factors are the same for all O+O and Ne+Ne centralities. Separate weights are obtained for the nominal and
alternative track selections. The systematic uncertainty associated with secondary particles is estimated
from the difference between results obtained with the two selections, each using its own set of weights.
In the data analysis, a per-track correction weight that accounts for efficiency, fake tracks, and secondary
particles is computed according to
𝑤corr = 1 −( 𝑓fake + 𝑓sec)

Example results for the reconstruction efficiency 𝜀and the combined fake- and secondary-track fraction
𝑓fake + 𝑓sec, obtained for the nominal track-selection working point are shown in Figure 1 as functions
of 𝑝T for two 𝜂intervals and two 𝑁rec
chg intervals. Figure 1 also shows the bin-by-bin correction factor
𝐶bbb, which is discussed later and corrects for migration of the reconstructed tracks in 𝑝T and 𝜂. The
reconstruction efficiency increases significantly with increasing 𝑝T at low 𝑝T, but increases slowly with 𝑝T
for 𝑝T ≳0.7 GeV. The combined fake- and secondary-track rates fall rapidly with increasing 𝑝T at low
𝑝T, primarily due to the 𝑝T dependence of the fake-track fractions. At higher 𝑝T, the 𝑓fake + 𝑓sec value is
dominated by secondary tracks, whose fraction varies slowly with 𝑝T.
Aside from the effects of reconstruction efficiency, fake tracks, and secondary tracks, the measurements are
affected by migration of the measured tracks in 𝑝T and 𝜂. The 𝑝T resolution of the ID is dominated by
multiple scattering in the 𝑝T range of the measurement, so 𝜎𝑝T/𝑝T varies slowly with 𝑝T. For |𝜂| < 1,
𝜎𝑝T/𝑝T decreases from 3% at 𝑝T = 0.2 GeV to 2% at 0.4 GeV and is nearly constant for higher 𝑝T. At
the most forward pseudorapidities (|𝜂| > 2), 𝜎𝑝T/𝑝T decreases from ∼6% at 𝑝T = 0.2 GeV to 5% at
𝑝T = 0.4 GeV, above which it remains constant. The (absolute) 𝜂resolution, 𝜎𝜂, decreases with increasing
𝑝T. For |𝜂| < 1, 𝜎𝜂decreases from 0.012 at 𝑝T = 0.2 GeV to 0.002 at 𝑝T = 5 GeV, while for 2 < |𝜂| < 2.5
it decreases from 0.018 at 𝑝T = 0.2 GeV to 0.002 at 𝑝T = 5 GeV. Given the quoted 𝑝T and 𝜂resolutions
and the chosen 𝑝T and 𝜂intervals for the measurement, bin-by-bin corrections for migration in 𝑝T and 𝜂
are sufficient for the measurement. Thus, instead of applying the corrections in Eq. (2) and unfolding the
measurements, simple bin-by-bin correction weights
𝑤bbb = 1 −( 𝑓fake + 𝑓sec)

𝑁rec
chg. Example results for 𝐶bbb are shown in Figure 1.

## Page 9


The measurements use 20 linear intervals and 36 logarithmic intervals in 𝜂and 𝑝T, respectively, spanning
the fiducial ranges. The measurements as a function of 𝑦𝜋use the same intervals as 𝜂, but results are not
reported in the first and last interval, owing to the loss of 𝑦𝜋acceptance.
Applications of the above-described analysis procedure to the reconstructed HIJING MC sample show
closure of the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂, ⟨𝑝T⟩, and double-differential yields to ≪1% except in the 0–5% centrality interval,
where the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂yield exhibits a ∼0.5% degree of 𝜂-dependent nonclosure. This nonclosure arises from
the finite multiplicity binning of the correction factors and is included in the systematic uncertainties of the
𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measurements.
4.2 Rapidity variables and impact on measurements
The physics of particle production in hadronic or nuclear collisions is most naturally expressed in terms of
particle rapidity (𝑦). The difference between 𝜂and 𝑦depends on 𝑝T such that 𝑦values migrate “outward”,
to larger |𝜂| values, at low 𝑝T, with the migration larger for increasing particle masses. The migration of
particles away from mid-rapidity leads to the well-known depletion near 𝜂∼0 in measurements of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂
in symmetric hadron–hadron or nucleus–nucleus collisions. Since the migration is largest at low 𝑝T, it also
has a significant impact on ⟨𝑝T⟩. Because most of the produced particles in hadronic collisions are pions,
the use of 𝑦𝜋as an alternative to 𝜂removes much of the distortion present in 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩(𝜂).
Figure 2 demonstrates the above considerations and the impact of the differences among the three rapidity
variables, 𝜂, 𝑦𝜋, and 𝑦, based on comparisons using the HIJING sample and a sample generated with the
EPOS event generator [68]. The figure compares 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂, 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦𝜋and 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦for primary charged particles
with 0 < 𝑝T < 5 GeV, produced by the generators in 5.36 TeV O+O collisions in the 0–5% centrality

## Page 10


interval. Comparisons between the ⟨𝑝T⟩values as a function of 𝜂, 𝑦𝜋and 𝑦are also shown. As the figure
demonstrates, 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂is smaller than 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦everywhere, but especially so near 𝜂= 0. The 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦𝜋values
are also smaller than 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦, but the maximum reduction is only ∼5% compared to a ∼20% reduction
in 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂. The ⟨𝑝T⟩evaluated as a function of 𝜂is significantly higher – by as much as 50 MeV – than
that evaluated using true rapidity owing to the migration of low-𝑝T particles away from 𝜂∼0. However,
the ⟨𝑝T⟩evaluated as a function of 𝑦𝜋is much closer to ⟨𝑝T⟩(𝑦), differing by less than 5 MeV. These
observations justify the use of 𝑦𝜋for some of the measurements presented in this paper.
The impact of using different rapidity variables when evaluating the invariant double-differential yields is
illustrated in Figure 3, which show ratios of (i) 𝑌𝜂(𝑝T) to 𝑌𝑦(𝑝T) ≡(1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝑦, (ii) 𝑌𝑦𝜋(𝑝T) to
𝑌𝑦(𝑝T), and (iii) 𝑌𝜂(𝑝T) to 𝑌𝑦𝜋(𝑝T). Results are shown for HIJING at the top and EPOS at the bottom for
three intervals of 𝜂, 𝑦𝜋, or 𝑦. The effect of the migration in 𝜂can be clearly observed at low 𝑝T, where the
𝑌𝜂(𝑝T) distribution is strongly suppressed relative to 𝑌𝑦(𝑝T). The 𝑌𝜂(𝑝T)-to-𝑌𝑦𝜋(𝑝T) ratio shows a similar
suppression at low 𝑝T, which means that the pions – or the effects of the small pion mass – dominate the
migration responsible for the suppression of 𝑌𝜂(𝑝T) at low 𝑝T. That conclusion has important implications
for this measurement, namely that the bulk of the distortion of 𝑌𝜂(𝑝T) can be obtained from the data by

The distortion of the 𝑝T spectra seen in Figure 3 must be accounted for when performing the 𝑝T > 0 MeV
extrapolation. Fortunately, the bulk of that distortion can be evaluated using the measured 𝑅𝜂,𝑦𝜋, which
greatly reduces potential sensitivity of the measurement to mismodeling of the data – particularly the flavor
distributions – in the HIJING MC simulation.
4.3 Extrapolation to zero transverse momentum
As described in the introduction, in addition to measuring 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩within the fiducial acceptance,
this measurement also includes an extrapolation to 𝑝T > 0 MeV. That extrapolation is performed by
fitting the 𝑝T dependence of 𝑌𝜂(𝑝T) or 𝑌𝑦𝜋(𝑝T) to a function that can be integrated either analytically or
numerically. Several functions, (𝐺(𝑝T)) based on either the Hagedorn function

closure when applied to the HIJING- and EPOS-generated primary charged-particle distributions in O+O
and Ne+Ne collisions, the 𝐺H+NE function provides better fits to the data, so it is used as the nominal
model for extrapolating to 𝑝T = 0, while results obtained with 𝐺T+NE are used for evaluating systematic
uncertainties.

To better illustrate the quality of the fits, Figure 6 shows results for (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂in O+O and
Ne+Ne collisions in 0–5% and 70–80% centrality intervals using “symmetrized” pseudorapidity intervals
|𝜂| < 0.25 (top) and 2.0 < |𝜂| < 2.25 (bottom) and ratios of the fit function to the data. The ratios differ
from unity by <2% at low 𝑝T, but show a systematic deviation from the data above 3 GeV. That deviation,
however, has negligible impact on the extrapolated 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩results.

𝜂(𝑝T), for the

where the 𝑝T factors in the integral are needed to convert from invariant differential yields to, e.g.,
𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂. The quantity 𝐹extr represents the multiplicative factor needed to correct the fiducial 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂
to the full, extrapolated 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂, while Δextr is an additive correction needed to convert the fiducial ⟨𝑝T⟩
to the full extrapolated result. In both 𝐹extr and Δextr, the normalization of 𝑓fit
𝜂cancels out in the ratio.
Systematic variations of 𝐹extr and Δextr associated with the extrapolation procedure are made by including
the MC-motivated 𝛾term in ℎ(𝑝T) (see Eq. (7)) or not, by varying the 𝑎parameter within statistical
uncertainties, by varying 𝐶(𝜂) within the uncertainty in the Gaussian smoothing function as described
above, and by including the 𝑅𝑦𝜋,𝑦(𝑝T) obtained from HIJING or setting that term to unity in Eq. (8). These
variations are found to change the 𝐹extr values by at most a few tenths of a percent and Δextr by less than a
few MeV. The inclusion of 𝑅𝜂,𝑦𝜋in Eq. (8) reduces 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and 𝐹extr by an 𝜂-dependent fraction that at
𝜂= 0 is ≲5% and increases ⟨𝑝T⟩and Δextr by a 𝜂-dependent shift that is ≲25 MeV.
Because O+O and Ne+Ne collision systems are intrinsically forward-backward symmetric, the corrections
to the fiducial measurements of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩, the 𝐹extr and Δextr values, should be symmetric in rapidity.
To ensure that symmetry, and to remove statistical fluctuations, 𝐹extr and Δextr are fitted to even polynomial
functions of 𝜂. However, the data with |𝜂| > 2.25 are excluded from the smoothing procedure because the
tracking performance is poorer at forward rapidities, leading to larger variations in 𝐹extr and Δextr that are
not well described by the even-polynomial fits. Examples of the results for 𝐹extr and Δextr obtained from
the aforementioned procedures, and the fits obtained to smooth the values, are shown in Figure 7.
5 Systematic uncertainties
Systematic uncertainties in the measurements arise primarily from potential inaccuracies in the corrections
for reconstruction efficiency, fake- and secondary-track rates, and bin migration. Such effects can originate
from mismodeling of the detector geometry and response in the MC simulations, imperfect modeling of
the true particle kinematic distributions in O+O or Ne+Ne collisions, differences between the detector
conditions during data-taking and those assumed in the MC simulation, or from the procedures used to
evaluate and apply the corrections. In addition to MC-based effects, the measurements can be affected
by uncertainties in the definition of the centrality intervals, biases associated with the trigger selection,
or background events passing the event selection. For results extrapolated to 𝑝T > 0 MeV, additional
uncertainties arise from the modeling of the extrapolation procedure. The evaluation of the systematic
uncertainties associated with these sources is described below.
i Track selection: The track selection criteria determine the relative contributions of primary charged
particles, secondary particles, and fake tracks entering the analysis. The results obtained with
the nominal selection are compared with those using the alternative track selections described in
Section 3. By varying the tracking requirements, the relative contributions of primary, secondary,
and fake tracks are modified, and the associated systematic uncertainty therefore covers the likely
spread of the contributions from secondary and fake tracks in the data. This uncertainty varies only
weakly with centrality and is taken to be symmetric about the nominal measurement.

Figure 7: Example results for (a, c) 𝐹extr and (b, d) Δextr obtained by fitting measured 𝑌𝜂(𝑝T) distributions to the
form in Eq. (8) and applying Eqs. (9). Results are shown for the (a, b) 0–1% and (c, d) 55–60% centrality intervals in
O+O collisions. The solid lines show the fits used to smooth and ensure that 𝐹extr and Δextr are symmetric around
𝜂= 0. The data with |𝜂| > 2.25 are excluded from the smoothing, as the tracking is poorer at forward rapidities.
ii Centrality definition: The uncertainty in the centrality determination over the 0–80% centrality
range is estimated to be ±2.2% (±2.5%) for the O+O (Ne+Ne) data. This uncertainty reflects
variations in the nuclear density profiles used in the Glauber fits, as well as additional sources such
as changes to the Σ𝐸FCAL
T
thresholds above which the fits are performed. The systematic uncertainty
associated with the centrality definition is evaluated by repeating the full analysis using alternative
centrality thresholds obtained from the Glauber study. This uncertainty exhibits a strong dependence
on centrality: it is smallest in the most central collisions and increases monotonically for increasingly
peripheral collisions. For the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measurements, this is the dominant systematic uncertainty
affecting the most peripheral centrality intervals.
iii Tracking efficiency: As mentioned in Section 4, the measurements use corrections to adjust the
tracking efficiencies and fake- and secondary-track rates that are obtained using MC simulations.
The sensitivity of the results to the modeling of the detector material and geometry is evaluated using
previously described alternative MC samples. The difference between the results obtained with
the alternative and nominal simulations is taken as the systematic uncertainty. The point-to-point
variations are assumed to be symmetric about the nominal result and are combined in quadrature. The
resulting fractional uncertainties are largely independent of centrality, but show a strong dependence
on 𝜂for the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measurement. At forward pseudorapidity (|𝜂| > 2), this source becomes one of
the dominant contributors to the total uncertainty in 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂.
iv MC reweighting: As described in Section 4, residual mismodeling of the particle kinematic
distributions in HIJING is addressed through 𝑝T-dependent and flavor-dependent reweighting of the
16


## Page 17


MC-generated charged-particle distributions. The reweighting factors are obtained from comparisons
with ALICE measurements of 𝐾/𝜋and 𝑝/𝜋𝑝T spectra in Xe+Xe collisions [67], and are applied
to kaons and protons in the HIJING simulation. The nominal analysis uses reweighting factors
obtained from the 50–60% centrality interval in Xe+Xe collisions. This Xe+Xe multiplicity roughly
corresponds to 0–10% centrality O+O collisions. Alternative reweighting factors are derived from
the 40–50% and 60–90% centrality intervals, and the resulting changes in the measured observables
are included as a systematic uncertainty.
v MC nonclosure: A closure test is performed by comparing the values of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩obtained
directly from generated particles in the MC simulation with those obtained by applying the full
reconstruction and analysis procedure to the simulated events. Closure is found to hold within
statistical uncertainties over the 5–80% centrality range. However, for the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measurement in
the most central collisions, a small degree of nonclosure is observed. This nonclosure amounts to
approximately 1% in the 0–1% centrality interval. A systematic uncertainty is therefore assigned
to the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measurement; it is zero above 5% centrality and increases linearly from 0% at 4–5%
centrality to 1% at 0–1% centrality. No nonclosure effect is observed for the ⟨𝑝T⟩measurement, and
no corresponding systematic uncertainty is assigned.
vi Fit model for 𝒑T > 0 MeV extrapolation: This uncertainty applies only to results extrapolated to
𝑝T > 0 MeV. It accounts for the dependence of the extrapolated measurements on the functional
form used to describe the 𝑝T spectra. As discussed in Section 4 the nominal results are obtained
using a “Hagedorn + negative exponential” parameterization. The change in the results when
using an alternative “Tsallis + negative exponential” parameterization is assigned as a systematic
uncertainty. This uncertainty is taken to be symmetric about the nominal value. It is one of the
leading uncertainties in the results after extrapolation to 𝑝T > 0 MeV.
vii Fit range for 𝒑T > 0 MeV extrapolation: This uncertainty also applies only to results extrapolated
to 𝑝T > 0 MeV. It accounts for the dependence of the extrapolation on the 𝑝T range used in the fit.
The nominal extrapolation uses fits to the 𝑝T spectra over the range 0.27–5.0 GeV. Alternative fit
ranges of 0.24–5.0 GeV and 0.27–3.9 GeV are used to assess this uncertainty.
viii 𝒚𝝅−𝜼weight: This uncertainty is another that applies only to results extrapolated to 𝑝T > 0 MeV.
The value of 𝑅fit
𝜂,𝑦𝜋(𝑝T) (Eq. (7)) is varied within its uncertainties, and the effect is propagated
through the analysis. This includes setting of the 𝛾term in Eq. (7) to zero, as discussed in Section 4.3.
Additionally, checks are done where the MC-determined 𝑅𝑦𝜋,𝑦term is removed from 𝑓fit
𝜂(𝑝T)
(Eq. (8)) and the analysis is repeated. The change in the final results is found to be negligible.
The total systematic uncertainty is obtained as the quadrature sum of the individual contributions. Figure 8
summarizes the systematic uncertainties for the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩measurements in O+O collisions for a
central and a peripheral centrality interval. The uncertainty associated with the chosen functional form of the
fit model is typically the leading systematic uncertainty. However, in peripheral collisions, the uncertainty
from the centrality determination also contributes significantly. Figure 9 shows the centrality dependence
of the systematic uncertainties at mid-rapidity. Figure 9 also shows the systematic uncertainties in the
Ne+Ne/O+O ratios of the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩spectra, where correlated uncertainties cancel out. Uncertainties
not associated with the extrapolation also apply to the 𝑝T > 0.27 GeV fiducial measurements.

## Page 19


6 Results
This section presents a subset of the results – in most cases for a limited set of centrality intervals – of the
measurements performed using the procedures described in the preceding sections. Results not included
here are presented in the appendix.
6.1 Theoretical calculations
The measurements presented in this paper are compared with theoretical calculations from Ref. [71]
(labeled as “IPGlasma”) and from Ref. [31] (labeled as “Trento”). They are also compared with events
generated with the EPOS model [68]. The following paragraphs describe each calculation in more detail.
The “IPGlasma” calculation combines the Projected Generator Coordinate Method (PGCM) [72], an
ab initio nuclear-structure model for oxygen and neon, with the IPGlasma framework [73] to compute
the initial energy densities of the colliding nuclei. These energy densities are evolved using viscous
relativistic hydrodynamics implemented in MUSIC [6]. A hadronic afterburner based on UrQMD [74,
75] is subsequently applied to obtain the final particle spectra and multiplicities. The estimated theory
uncertainties in the IPGlasma calculations are purely statistical.
The “Trento” calculations are described in Ref. [31], and use either the PGCM [72] or the Nuclear Lattice
Effective Field Theory (NLEFT) simulations [61, 72] to determine the nuclear configurations of oxygen and
neon. These configurations are used to generate initial-state energy densities using the Trento model [35].
The resulting events are evolved using the Trajectum hydrodynamic framework [62, 76] to obtain the final
particle spectra and multiplicity distributions. The estimated theory uncertainties in the Trento calculations
include statistical uncertainties from finite event sampling and systematic uncertainties obtained by sampling
multiple parameter sets from a Bayesian posterior constrained by Pb+Pb data for several observables (see
Refs. [31, 77]), as well as variations arising from finite grid spacing and nuclear-structure modeling.
The EPOS calculations are performed using version 4.0.3 of the EPOS model in its core–corona
configuration [78–82] including hydrodynamic modeling. Initial particle production is described within a
parton-based Gribov–Regge multiple scattering framework, with parton ladders hadronizing via string
fragmentation. The produced matter is separated into a dense core, which undergoes hydrodynamic
evolution, and a dilute corona, which hadronizes without significant final-state interactions. Final-state
hadronic rescatterings are included through a hadronic cascade. The estimated theory uncertainties in the
EPOS calculations are purely statistical.
In the figures shown in this section, the statistical and systematic uncertainties of the Trento model
calculations are combined in quadrature and shown as total uncertainties for clarity. Since the IPGlasma
and EPOS model calculations include only statistical uncertainties, their displayed total uncertainties are
purely statistical.
The Trento calculations are available only for the 𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolated case, and are not shown when
comparing with the 𝑝T > 270 MeV fiducial measurements, except when comparing ratios where the effect
of the fiducial acceptance largely cancels. Similarly, IPGlasma calculations for the 𝑝T spectra are not
available in 1%-wide centrality intervals and are not shown when comparing with the 𝑝T spectra measured
in 1%-wide centrality intervals.

Figure 10: The measured 𝑝T spectra in (a, c, e) O+O and (b, d, f) Ne+Ne collisions at mid-rapidity for the (a, b)
0–1%, (c, d) 20–25% and (e, f) 50–55% centrality intervals. For comparison, predictions from models described in

20


## Page 21


6.2 Transverse momentum distributions
Figure 10 presents the double-differential invariant yields, (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂, measured in O+O (left)
and Ne+Ne (right) collisions in three centrality intervals and also shows the corresponding theoretical
distributions. Figure 11 presents the ratios of the theory predictions to the data. Figures 28–29 and 30–31
in the Appendix respectively show invariant yields and ratios of theory predictions to data in additional
centrality intervals. In general, the 𝑝T spectra in the data are qualitatively reproduced by the theory
calculations, especially at low 𝑝T. For 𝑝T < 2 GeV, the Trento calculations are within about ±15% of the
data, but show a systematic deviation with increasing 𝑝T that is more pronounced in the more peripheral
collisions. In contrast, the IPGlasma and EPOS ratios to data are nearly 𝑝T-independent and within about
±5% of unity for 𝑝T ≲1 GeV. Both the Trento and IPGlasma calculations underestimate the data for
𝑝T ≳2 GeV, as does EPOS but to a lesser degree. The failure of the Trento and IPGlasma calculations
to describe the data at high 𝑝T presumably arises because the hydrodynamic models do not include hard
processes. Hard processes are included in EPOS, but the hard-scattering rates may be insufficient to
describe the data.
Figure 12 shows the ratio of the Ne+Ne to O+O 𝑝T spectra, together with comparisons with theoretical
calculations. Figure 32 in the Appendix shows similar results for additional centrality intervals. Clear
differences between data and the theoretical predictions are seen in the magnitude of the ratios, indicating
deficiencies in the description of the (relative) overall particle yield. These are discussed further below.
The measured Ne+Ne to O+O ratios are found to be nearly independent of 𝑝T, with the ratios varying
by <10% over the measured range. However, in the more central collisions – i.e. in the top panels – a
slow decrease in the ratio with increasing 𝑝T is seen, while in the more peripheral intervals – bottom
panels – a slight increase in the ratio with increasing 𝑝T is observed at low 𝑝T. The EPOS calculations
generally reproduce the trends seen in the measurements, except in the 20–25% centrality interval. In
contrast, the IPGlasma- and Trento-based hydrodynamic model calculations exhibit a much stronger 𝑝T
dependence in the Ne+Ne to O+O ratios than is observed in the data, with the NLEFT and PGCM Trento
calculations showing opposite trends. In central collisions, the data lie within the range spanned by the
NLEFT and PGCM Trento predictions, suggesting that neither of the Trento models provides the correct
initial conditions for the hydrodynamic evolution. In the most peripheral centrality interval shown in the
figure, the hydrodynamic calculations disagree with the measurements but exhibit a weak 𝑝T dependence
similar to that observed in the data.
Figure 13 shows the ratios of the (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂spectra in O+O collisions at various centralities to that
measured in the 70–80% peripheral centrality interval. For these ratios, the spectra are first normalized by
the 𝑝T-integrated multiplicity in the corresponding 𝜂or 𝑦𝜋interval. This normalization removes the overall
change in particle production between the centrality intervals, allowing the ratios to emphasize changes
in the shape of the 𝑝T spectra. Figures 33 and 34 in the Appendix show similar results for additional
centrality intervals in O+O collisions. Figures 35 and 36 in the Appendix show similar results for Ne+Ne
collisions. From these ratios, it is observed that when going from peripheral to central collisions, the
spectra become progressively harder, with the yields below 𝑝T ∼0.5 GeV suppressed in the more central
collisions, and the yields at higher 𝑝T enhanced. However, the enhancement then decreases with increasing
𝑝T such that by 5 GeV the ratios have little centrality dependence. This trend is observed in both the
𝜂-binned measurements and the 𝑦𝜋-binned measurements, and at both mid-rapidity and forward rapidity.
The observed behavior is consistent with the development of stronger radial flow in the more central O+O
collisions combined with the dominance of hard processes at the upper end of the measured 𝑝T range.
To more directly test the rapidity dependence of the hardening of the 𝑝T spectra, Figure 14 shows the ratios

of the (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂(left) and (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝑦𝜋(right) spectra at 0–1% and 30–40% centrality
to those at 70–80% centrality at mid-rapidity (|𝜂, 𝑦𝜋| < 0.5) and forward rapidity (1.5 < |𝜂, 𝑦𝜋| < 2.5).
Results for additional centrality intervals are shown in the Appendix, in Figures 37–38 for O+O collisions
and in Figures 39–40 for Ne+Ne collisions. The (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂and (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝑦𝜋ratios are
consistent, indicating that the bias in the 𝜂distributions at low 𝑝T cancels out in the ratios. The mid-rapidity
and forward-rapidity ratios are consistent within uncertainties, indicating that the radial flow developed in
the O+O collisions is empirically observed to be approximately boost-invariant over |𝜂| < 2.5.
Figure 15 compares the measured (1/𝑝T) 𝑑2𝑛/𝑑𝑝T 𝑑𝜂ratios with those obtained from the model calculations.
Figures 41 and 42 in the Appendix show similar results for additional centrality intervals for O+O and
Ne+Ne collisions, respectively. In general, the ratios in the model calculations show a stronger dependence
on 𝑝T than observed in the data. The EPOS ratios reach a maximum and then decrease with increasing
𝑝T as in the data, but the maximum values are substantially higher than in the data except in the most
peripheral (65–70%) centrality interval. The hydrodynamic calculations keep increasing monotonically,
probably because of the lack of hard contributions.

6.3 Particle yields and mean transverse momenta
Results of the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦𝜋measurements are shown in Figure 16 for O+O and Ne+Ne collisions
in several representative centrality intervals. Results are presented for the fiducial region 𝑝T > 270 MeV
as well as for the 𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolation. Results for additional centrality intervals are presented in
Figures 43–46 in the Appendix. The 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂distributions exhibit the expected dependence on pseudorapidity.
A dip is observed at mid-pseudorapidity, which arises from presenting the distributions as a function of
pseudorapidity rather than rapidity (see Section 4.2). This dip is more pronounced for the 𝑝T > 0 MeV
extrapolation and is largely removed when the measurements are plotted as a function of 𝑦𝜋. The ⟨𝑝T⟩
measurements as a function of 𝜂and 𝑦𝜋are shown in Figure 17 for both the 𝑝T > 270 MeV fiducial region
and the 𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolation. Results for additional centrality intervals are presented in Figures 47–50
in the Appendix. The ⟨𝑝T⟩values peak at mid-(pseudo)rapidity and decrease toward forward rapidities. In
addition, ⟨𝑝T⟩increases monotonically when going from peripheral to central collisions. The 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂values
obtained from the 𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolation are approximately 1.4 times larger than the corresponding
fiducial measurements. In contrast, the ⟨𝑝T⟩values from the 𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolation are approximately
150–200 MeV lower than the fiducial measurements, reflecting the inclusion of low-𝑝T particles in the

Figure 19: Comparison of the measured 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂with model calculations for (a, c) O+O and (b, d) Ne+Ne collisions.
Panels (a, b) correspond to the 𝑝T > 0.27 GeV fiducial region, and panels (c, d) to the 𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolation. The
EPOS calculations are shown over the full 𝜂range, while the IPGlasma- and Trento-based hydrodynamic calculations
are averaged over |𝜂| < 0.5 and displayed only in this region.
extrapolated spectra. Across the measured 𝜂range, the ⟨𝑝T⟩variation is ∼100 MeV for the 𝑝T > 0 MeV
case. In comparison, the 𝑦𝜋-dependent measurements shown in Figures 17(e) and 17(f) exhibit a much
weaker dependence, varying by only ∼30 MeV over the measured 𝑦𝜋range. As discussed previously, this
difference arises from the kinematic mapping between rapidity and pseudorapidity. Particles at a given
|𝑦𝜋| correspond to larger |𝜂| values, with the migration being more pronounced at low 𝑝T. Consequently,
low-𝑝T particles shift further outward when plotted versus 𝜂, which increases ⟨𝑝T⟩and reduces 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂near
|𝜂| ∼0, leading to the stronger variation in the results plotted as a function of 𝜂. This effect is clearly shown
in Figure 18, where the 𝜂- and 𝑦𝜋-dependent measurements are plotted together. Similar comparisons for
additional centrality intervals, as well as for the 𝑝T > 0.27 GeV fiducial measurements, are included in the
Appendix in Figures 51–54 for O+O collisions, and in Figures 55–58 for Ne+Ne collisions.
Figure 19 shows comparisons of the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measurements with calculations from the EPOS model.
Comparisons with the IPGlasma- and Trento-based hydrodynamic calculations, averaged over |𝜂| < 0.5
are also shown. Similar comparisons for additional centrality intervals in O+O collisions are shown in
Figure 59 and for Ne+Ne collisions are shown in Figure 60 in the Appendix. Figure 20 shows data–EPOS
comparisons for ⟨𝑝T⟩as a function of 𝜂. Similar comparisons in additional centrality intervals are shown

for O+O collisions in Figure 61 and are shown for Ne+Ne collisions in Figure 62 in the Appendix. In
general, the calculations qualitatively reproduce the trends seen in the measurements. More detailed
quantitative data–model comparisons for the centrality dependence of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩at mid-rapidity
(|𝜂| < 0.5) are discussed in Section 6.4.
6.4 Centrality dependence of particle yields and mean transverse momenta
Figure 21 shows the centrality dependence of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measured at mid-rapidity (|𝜂| < 0.5) for the
𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolation. Comparisons with model calculations are also included. As is typical in
nucleus–nucleus collisions, the charged-particle multiplicity rises most steeply toward the most central
collisions. Overall, the models reproduce the observed centrality dependence reasonably well. For both the
O+O and Ne+Ne collisions, the Trento-based hydrodynamic calculations agree with the measurements
within ∼1𝜎uncertainties over the 0–60% centrality range. The IPGlasma-based hydrodynamic calculations
and the EPOS model show even better agreement with the data. This is illustrated by the theory-to-data
ratios shown in the lower panels of Figure 21. In peripheral collisions, noticeable discrepancies between

Figure 21: The centrality dependence of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measured at mid-rapidity (|𝜂| < 0.5) in (a) O+O and (b) Ne+Ne
collisions. Comparisons with calculations from models described in Ref. [71] (labeled “IPGlasma”) and Ref. [31]
(labeled “Trento”) as well as the EPOS model are also shown. Theory-to-data ratios are shown for (c) O+O and (d)
Ne+Ne collisions. For the data in panels (a, b), the vertical lines and shaded bars indicate statistical and systematic
uncertainties, respectively, and in all cases are smaller than the size of the data points. For the theory points in panels
(a, b) and the theory-to-data ratios in panels (c, d), the shaded bands indicate combined (statistical + systematic)
uncertainties.
data and the Trento- and IPGlasma-based model calculations emerge. These differences are larger for
O+O than for Ne+Ne collisions, and are more pronounced for the Trento model than for the IPGlasma
model. Part of the discrepancy may arise from the different rapidity regions used to define centrality in the
data and in the Trento- and IPGlasma-based hydrodynamic model calculations. In the data, centrality is
determined using forward-calorimeter transverse energy. For the EPOS model comparisons, the centrality
was determined using the generated final particles in the FCal acceptance. However, the Trento and
IPGlasma based model calculations shown here determine centrality by using charged-particle multiplicity
at mid-rapidity. Similar comparisons for the 𝑝T > 0.27 GeV fiducial region are shown in Figure 63 in the
Appendix. The fiducial measurements show similar trends.
Figure 22 shows the centrality dependence of ⟨𝑝T⟩at mid-rapidity (|𝜂| < 0.5) for the 𝑝T > 0 MeV
extrapolation, together with model comparisons. In contrast to the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂results, the ⟨𝑝T⟩values are nearly
identical for O+O and Ne+Ne collisions. At a given centrality, the differences between the two systems are
within 0.5% (less than 5 MeV) over the full measured centrality range. The ⟨𝑝T⟩decreases monotonically
from central to peripheral collisions, with a total variation of ∼100 MeV across the centrality range. The

Figure 22: The centrality dependence of ⟨𝑝T⟩measured at mid-rapidity (|𝜂| < 0.5) in (a) O+O and (b) Ne+Ne
collisions. Comparisons with calculations from the EPOS model and models described in Ref. [71] (labeled
“IPGlasma”) and Ref. [31] (labeled “Trento”) are also shown. Theory-to-data ratios are shown for (c) O+O and (d)
Ne+Ne collisions. For the data in panels (a, b), the vertical lines and vertical bands indicate statistical and systematic

model calculations reproduce the observed centrality dependence well and typically agree with the data
within ±5%. The Trento-based hydrodynamic calculations predict ⟨𝑝T⟩values slightly higher than those
observed in the data. The EPOS model calculations transition from higher values than in data in central
collisions to lower values in peripheral collisions. The IPGlasma-based hydrodynamic calculations agree
best with the measurements. Similar comparisons for the 𝑝T > 0.27 GeV fiducial region are shown in
Figure 64 in the Appendix. The fiducial measurements show similar trends.
Figure 23 shows the dependence of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩measured at mid-rapidity on the number of participating
nucleons in the collisions, ⟨𝑁part⟩. For ⟨𝑁part⟩values below approximately 25, corresponding to the 10%
and more peripheral centrality intervals, the measured (1/⟨𝑁part⟩) 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂in O+O and Ne+Ne are consistent,
within uncertainties. In this region, the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂dependence is approximately linear in ⟨𝑁part⟩. At larger
⟨𝑁part⟩, the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩values rise more sharply as the maximum 𝑁part values are approached. This
rise is consistent with the behavior observed in ultracentral collisions in Pb+Pb and Xe+Xe collisions [16,
83], where for small impact parameters the transverse area of the medium becomes constant, but initial-state
fluctuations increase the energy density leading to enhanced radial flow and, thus, higher ⟨𝑝T⟩. Figure 24

Figure 23: The ⟨𝑁part⟩dependence of (a) (1/⟨𝑁part⟩)𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and (b) ⟨𝑝T⟩measured at mid-rapidity (|𝜂| < 0.5).
The vertical lines and shaded bars indicate statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively. In panel (a), the

determination. Statistical uncertainties in all cases are smaller than the size of the data points.
compares the ⟨𝑁part⟩dependence of the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩measurements at mid-rapidity with model
calculations. The trends observed here are similar to those in Figures 21 and 22: for 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂, the models are
closer to the data in central collisions but underestimate it in peripheral collisions, while for ⟨𝑝T⟩, the data
lie between the different model predictions.
Figure 25 shows the Ne+Ne/O+O ratios for 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂, 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦𝜋, and ⟨𝑝T⟩measured at mid-rapidity, for
results extrapolated to 𝑝T > 0 MeV. Figures 65–68 in the Appendix show similar comparisons for the
𝑝T > 0.27 GeV fiducial region, together with the measured 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂, 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝑦𝜋, and ⟨𝑝T⟩values, as a function
of centrality. For 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂, the ratio is approximately 1.2 in 0–1% centrality collisions, increases slightly
over the 0–5% centrality range, and then decreases monotonically to approximately 1.04 by the 75–80%
centrality interval. Comparisons with model calculations are also shown in Figure 25. In the IPGlasma- and
Trento-based hydrodynamic model calculations, the increase in the ratio over the 0–5% centrality collisions
is significantly larger, particularly for the IPGlasma model, and persists over the 0–20% centrality range.
The subsequent decrease toward peripheral collisions is substantially weaker than that observed in the data.
The EPOS model calculations are consistent with the data throughout the measured multiplicity range. For
the ⟨𝑝T⟩ratios, the data exhibit variations typically less than ±1% across the measured centrality range.
The measured ratios lie between the different model predictions. The results for |𝑦𝜋| < 0.5 are nearly
identical to the measurements for |𝜂| < 0.5. For these ratios, the measurements for the 𝑝T > 0.27 GeV
fiducial region are also nearly identical to the 𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolation case as shown in Figures 65–68.
The uncertainty in these measured ratios is smaller than the differences between predictions obtained with
different nuclear-structure models (PGCM and NLEFT), indicating that these measurements are sensitive
to the modeling of the nuclear structure.

36


## Page 37


7 Conclusion
This paper presents measurements of charged-particle 𝑝T spectra, multiplicity, and mean transverse
momentum in O+O and Ne+Ne collisions at 5.36 TeV per nucleon pair, using the ATLAS detector at the
LHC. The integrated luminosities of the O+O and Ne+Ne data sets used in this measurement are 27.7 𝜇b−1
and 53.1 𝜇b−1, respectively. The measurements are performed using charged-particle tracks reconstructed
in the inner detector over the fiducial phase space 0.27 < 𝑝T < 5 GeV and |𝜂| < 2.5. Results are reported as
a function of pseudorapidity and collision centrality, where the latter is characterized by the total transverse
energy measured in the forward calorimeters. Reconstruction efficiencies and fake- and secondary-track
contributions are corrected using Monte Carlo simulations, and a comprehensive evaluation of systematic
uncertainties is performed. Systematic uncertainties in the measurements arise from the track selection,
detector material description, centrality determination, and procedure for extrapolation to 𝑝T > 0 MeV.
Measurements are also performed as a function of rapidity 𝑦𝜋computed for the charged-pion mass
hypothesis. The use of 𝑦𝜋significantly reduces effects arising from the difference between pseudorapidity
and true rapidity, particularly near mid-rapidity, providing results closer to those that would be obtained
using true rapidity. The dynamics of particle production are expected to be weakly dependent on rapidity, a
feature reflected in the measurements presented as a function of 𝑦𝜋.
Ratios of the 𝑝T spectra between central and peripheral collisions are measured. These indicate a hardening
of the 𝑝T spectra from peripheral to central collisions for 𝑝T ≲2 GeV, and demonstrate the buildup of
radial flow. This buildup is found to be similar at mid-rapidity and forward rapidity, consistent with the
approximate boost invariance of radial flow. Measurements of the Ne+Ne/O+O ratio of the spectra for a
given centrality are found to be nearly independent of 𝑝T.
The charged-particle multiplicity and mean transverse momentum are both measured within the fiducial
acceptance and extrapolated to 𝑝T > 0 MeV, using fits to the measured 𝑝T spectra. The results show
a smooth evolution of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩with centrality and (pseudo-)rapidity in both collision systems.
For a given centrality interval, Ne+Ne collisions exhibit larger charged-particle multiplicities than O+O
collisions. The Ne+Ne/O+O ratio of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂values varies between approximately 1.05 and 1.20, while the
corresponding ratio of ⟨𝑝T⟩values varies between approximately 0.99 and 1.01. These ratios are consistent
between the 𝜂- and 𝑦𝜋-based measurements. The Ne+Ne/O+O ratios are also found to be consistent
between the fiducial and 𝑝T > 0 MeV measurements. The multiplicities and ⟨𝑝T⟩exhibit a significantly
weaker dependence as a function of 𝑦𝜋than as a function of 𝜂, reflecting the approximate boost invariance
of particle production.
The measurements are compared with hydrodynamic model calculations in which the initial energy
densities are computed using the IPGlasma and Trento frameworks. These frameworks incorporate
state-of-the-art nuclear-structure models for oxygen and neon to provide initial conditions for simulations
of the quark–gluon plasma evolution. The IPGlasma calculations are coupled to the MUSIC hydrodynamic
package, while the Trento calculations use the Trajectum package. Comparisons with the EPOS event
generator are also made. The model calculations generally reproduce the observed trends, but exhibit some
deviations from the data that increase for peripheral collisions. Among the models considered, EPOS
provides the best overall agreement with the data, while IPGlasma-based predictions show slightly better
agreement than those based on Trento. Comparisons of the Ne+Ne/O+O ratios for the 𝑝T spectra, 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂
and ⟨𝑝T⟩with Trento calculations using the PGCM and NLEFT nuclear-structure models demonstrate that
the measurements presented here provide sensitivity to differences between nuclear-structure models.
37


## Page 38


These measurements constitute the first detailed ATLAS results for charged-particle production in O+O
and Ne+Ne collisions at the LHC. They provide important experimental constraints on models of particle
production and collective behavior in small- and intermediate-sized nuclear systems, and complement
existing measurements in 𝑝𝑝, 𝑝+A, and heavy-ion collisions. Together, the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂and ⟨𝑝T⟩measurements
reported here provide a baseline for understanding particle production and collective effects in light-ion
collisions, and contribute to a more complete picture of the onset and evolution of quark–gluon plasma
dynamics as a function of system size.

Appendix
Figure 26 shows the ratios of the 𝑝T spectra measured in |𝜂| < 0.5 to those measured in 1.5 < |𝜂| < 2.25, for
O+O and Ne+Ne collisions. Figure 27 shows the corresponding ratios for spectra measured in intervals of
𝑦𝜋, comparing |𝑦𝜋| < 0.5 with 1.5 < |𝑦𝜋| < 2.25. In Figure 26, a depletion is observed at 𝑝T ≲0.6 GeV,
which is absent in Figure 27. This depletion is an artifact of presenting the results in intervals of 𝜂. It
arises from the 𝜂-migration discussed in Section 4.2, which is enhanced at low 𝑝T and near 𝜂∼0, and
modifies the shape of the 𝑝T spectra.
The remaining figures and tables in the Appendix support results shown in the main body of the paper and
are referenced there; they are therefore not discussed further here.

systematic uncertainties, respectively. For the theory points, the shaded bands indicate total uncertainties.

Figure 63: The centrality dependence of the 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂measured at mid-rapidity (|𝜂| < 0.5) in (a) O+O and (b) Ne+Ne
collisions for the 𝑝T > 270 MeV fiducial range. Comparisons with EPOS and calculations from models described in
Ref. [71] (labeled “IPGlasma”) are also shown. Theory-to-data ratios are shown for (c) O+O and (d) Ne+Ne collisions.
For the data in panels (a, b), the vertical lines and vertical bars indicate statistical and systematic uncertainties,
respectively. Statistical uncertainties in all cases are smaller than the size of the data points. For the theory points
shown in panels (a, b), and the theory-to-data ratios shown in (c, d), the shaded bands indicate combined (statistical +
systematic) uncertainties.

Figure 65: Comparison of the centrality dependence of 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂in O+O and Ne+Ne collisions for the (a) 𝑝T > 270 MeV
fiducial region and the (c) 𝑝T > 0 MeV extrapolation. Panels (b, d) show the centrality dependence of the Ne+Ne/O+O
ratio for 𝑑𝑛/𝑑𝜂. Results are for measurements at mid-rapidity (|𝜂| < 0.5). Panels (b, d) also show comparisons with
EPOS and calculations from models described in Ref. [71] (labeled “IPGlasma”) and Ref. [31] (labeled “Trento”). For
the data, the vertical lines and vertical bars indicate statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively. Statistical
uncertainties in all cases are smaller than the size of the data points. For the theory points, the error bars indicate
combined (statistical+systematic) uncertainties. The Trento model calculations are always for the 𝑝T > 0 MeV case,
and are identical in panels (b, d). Panel (d) is identical to Figure 25a.
